### Question 50: Stewart-Treves Syndrome
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of Stewart-Treves syndrome, which is a rare complication characterized by the development of angiosarcoma in the setting of chronic lymphedema, typically following radical mastectomy with axillary lymph node dissection. (斯图尔特-特雷维斯综合征，一种罕见的并发症，其特征是在慢性淋巴水肿的背景下发展成血管肉瘤，通常发生在根治性乳房切除术和腋窝淋巴结清扫术之后。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old woman presents to the dermatology clinic complaining of new skin lesions on her left arm. Twelve years prior, she underwent a radical mastectomy and complete axillary lymph node dissection for invasive ductal carcinoma of the left breast. Since that surgery, she has experienced persistent, non-pitting edema of her left arm, which has worsened over the past year. Over the last six months, she has noticed multiple, painless, blue-purple, bruise-like nodules appearing on the edematous skin of her left arm. These nodules have gradually increased in size. Physical examination reveals severe lymphedema of the left arm, characterized by thickened, non-pitting skin and subcutaneous fibrosis. Multiple, raised, violaceous papules and nodules, ranging from 1 to 3 cm in diameter, are present on the dorsal and volar surfaces of the forearm and upper arm. A biopsy of one of the nodules shows poorly differentiated, infiltrating spindle cells forming anastomosing vascular channels lined by atypical endothelial cells. Immunohistochemical staining reveals strong positivity for CD31 and ERG within the tumor cells. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis?

(A) Kaposi sarcoma
(B) Metastatic breast carcinoma
(C) Lymphangiosarcoma secondary to chronic lymphedema (Stewart-Treves syndrome)
(D) Cutaneous T-cell lymphoma (mycosis fungoides)
(E) Dermatofibrosarcoma protuberans

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Pathophysiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 68-year-old female with a history of left breast cancer treated with radical mastectomy and axillary lymph node dissection 12 years ago. This history is crucial as it points towards potential long-term complications related to the surgery and cancer treatment.
- **Chief Complaint:** New, painless, blue-purple, bruise-like nodules on the left arm. The description suggests a vascular lesion or a lesion with vascular involvement.
- **Past Medical History:** Radical mastectomy and axillary lymph node dissection for breast cancer. This procedure disrupts lymphatic drainage, leading to chronic lymphedema.
- **Physical Exam:** Severe, chronic, non-pitting lymphedema of the left arm. This is the key predisposing factor. The presence of multiple violaceous papules and nodules confirms the skin lesions.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Poorly differentiated, infiltrating spindle cells forming anastomosing vascular channels. This histological description is characteristic of angiosarcoma.
- **Immunohistochemistry:** Strong positivity for CD31 and ERG. CD31 (PECAM-1) and ERG are endothelial markers, confirming the vascular origin of the tumor cells.
- **Synthesis:** The combination of long-standing chronic lymphedema following radical mastectomy and the development of a cutaneous angiosarcoma (poorly differentiated spindle cells forming vascular channels, positive for endothelial markers) points directly to Stewart-Treves syndrome.

##### 2. Pathophysiological Deep-Dive (病理生理机制深度剖析)
- **Initiating Event:** Radical mastectomy with axillary lymph node dissection disrupts the lymphatic drainage pathways of the arm.
- **Consequence:** This disruption leads to chronic lymphedema, characterized by the accumulation of protein-rich lymph fluid in the interstitial space.
- **Pathophysiology of Lymphedema:** Chronic lymphedema causes inflammation, fibrosis, and tissue hypoxia. The stagnant lymph fluid provides a growth factor-rich environment.
- **Molecular Changes:** Chronic inflammation and hypoxia can lead to genetic instability and mutations in endothelial cells within the lymphatic vessels or blood vessels of the affected limb.
- **Neoplastic Transformation:** Over time (often years to decades), these changes can lead to the malignant transformation of endothelial cells into angiosarcoma.
- **Stewart-Treves Syndrome Definition:** This specific entity is defined as angiosarcoma arising in the setting of chronic lymphedema, most commonly after radical mastectomy.
- **Histology:** The tumor consists of malignant endothelial cells forming atypical vascular channels, often infiltrating the dermis and subcutaneous tissue.
- **Immunohistochemistry:** Tumor cells express endothelial markers like CD31, Factor VIII-related antigen (von Willebrand factor), ERG, and FLI1.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Presents as purplish or reddish-blue nodules, papules, or plaques on the edematous limb, often painless initially but can become ulcerated and painful later. It is an aggressive malignancy with a poor prognosis.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Kaposi sarcoma:** This is a vascular neoplasm caused by Human Herpesvirus 8 (HHV-8), typically associated with immunosuppression (e.g., AIDS) or older age. Lesions are usually reddish-purple macules, papules, nodules, or plaques. Histologically, it shows spindle cells forming vascular channels, positive for HHV-8 LANA-1. While it shares some morphological similarities (vascular channels, purplish lesions), the clinical context (chronic lymphedema post-mastectomy) and the specific etiology (HHV-8) are different. The patient's history strongly points away from Kaposi sarcoma.
- (B) **Metastatic breast carcinoma:** Breast cancer can metastasize to the skin, often presenting as nodules or plaques. However, cutaneous metastases typically retain features of the primary tumor (e.g., ductal carcinoma in situ features, ER/PR/HER2 positivity). The histology described (poorly differentiated spindle cells forming vascular channels) is not typical for breast carcinoma metastasis, which usually shows epithelial cells. While metastasis is a concern in a breast cancer survivor, the specific histological and immunohistochemical findings are inconsistent with it.
- (D) **Cutaneous T-cell lymphoma (mycosis fungoides):** This is a malignancy of mature T-lymphocytes that primarily affects the skin. It typically presents with eczematous patches, plaques, or tumors. Histology shows atypical lymphoid infiltrates in the epidermis (Pautrier microabscesses) and dermis. Immunohistochemistry would show T-cell markers (CD3, CD4, CD8). The described histology (vascular channels, spindle cells, CD31+) is completely different from mycosis fungoides.
- (E) **Dermatofibrosarcoma protuberans (DFSP):** This is a rare, locally aggressive fibrohistiocytic tumor of the dermis. It typically presents as a firm, slowly growing plaque or nodule. Histologically, it shows a proliferation of spindle cells arranged in a storiform pattern, infiltrating the subcutaneous tissue. It is characterized by a specific translocation t(17;22) involving the COL1A1 and PDGFB genes. Immunohistochemistry is typically positive for CD34. The histology (vascular channels, endothelial markers) described in the vignette is inconsistent with DFSP.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Stewart-Treves syndrome is a rare but important complication of chronic lymphedema, most commonly following radical mastectomy. It represents angiosarcoma arising in this setting. Key features include long-standing lymphedema, development of purplish skin nodules, and histology/immunohistochemistry confirming angiosarcoma (vascular channels, CD31+, ERG+). Remember this association for board exams. Differential diagnoses for skin nodules in a patient with lymphedema include Kaposi sarcoma (if HHV-8 risk factors), metastatic disease, and other primary skin malignancies, but the specific context and pathology point strongly to Stewart-Treves syndrome.
